State lawmaker pay in 20 states, from New York's $142,000 to New Mexico's $0

📅 Nov 03, 2026  |  Category: Business  |  Read Source →

Free daily briefing on global business news.

State legislator salaries run from $142,000 in New York to $0 in New Mexico, and these 20 states show the range in base pay and per diems

A New York state lawmaker earns $142,000 a year. A New Hampshire lawmaker earns $100 a year and gets no per diem. New Mexico sits outside that scale entirely. It is the one state that sets no legislative salary, though its legislators collect a per diem of $202 a day. Those figures come from Ballotpedia's 2025 comparison of state legislative salaries. They show how far state legislator salaries spread across the U.S.

All five legislatures that Ballotpedia names as its top five by pay operate full time. Every state in the lower half of this selection runs a hybrid or part-time legislature.

States also differ in who decides the pay. Commissions play that role in 19 states. Some of those commissions produce reports that bind no one. Others make recommendations that take effect unless the legislature, the governor or voters block them. In Arizona and Nebraska, voters must approve any recommended change. Some states tie legislative pay to what other state employees earn. Others let lawmakers set their own pay. The National Conference of State Legislatures calls the political risk of voting yourself a raise the "pay problem." Inflation can then outrun that pay. When it does, real salaries shrink over time.

This slideshow covers 20 of the 50 states. It includes 10 from the upper end of the annual base salaries in Ballotpedia's 2025 table and 10 from the lower end. Ballotpedia names only a top five. The rest of the order here comes from sorting the table's annual figures. States that pay lawmakers by the day, week, month or session are left off both lists. Ballotpedia gives no session lengths, so their pay cannot be placed on an annual scale. Those states are Kentucky, Montana, Nevada, Utah, Wyoming, Vermont, North Dakota and Maine. Washington state and Wisconsin fall just below Alabama and outside this selection. Each slide gives a state's base salary, its per diem and whether its legislature is full time, hybrid or part time.

New York pays its state legislators an annual base salary of $142,000, according to Ballotpedia's 2025 comparison of state legislative salaries. That is the largest annual base salary among the 20 states covered here.

New York lawmakers also receive a per diem of $203 a day. The two payments work differently. The salary is a fixed yearly amount. It arrives regardless of how many days the legislature meets. The per diem is a daily payment, and the 2025 table lists it separately from base pay.

The table does not give the length of New York's legislative session. It also does not say how many days members claim the per diem. Without those figures, the daily rate cannot be turned into a reliable yearly total. The $203 is a separate daily payment, not an addition to the $142,000 salary.

Ballotpedia classifies the New York legislature as full time. The National Conference of State Legislatures (NCSL) uses the same category in a report dated July 28, 2021. In that report, lawmakers in full-time legislatures devote about 84% of a full-time job to legislative work. That share means legislative work takes up most of a standard working year for members in this group. Average pay in that group was about $82,358.

New York's $142,000 salary sits well above that full-time average. The two figures come from different years, however. Ballotpedia's number reflects 2025 pay. The NCSL average dates to its 2021 report. Pay in other full-time states may have changed since then. A current average could sit higher or lower than the 2021 figure. The comparison shows the scale of New York's pay rather than a precise difference.

The full-time label and the salary describe the same job from two angles. One measures how much of a working year the role takes. The other measures what the state pays for it.

California pays its state legislators a base salary of $132,703 a year. Lawmakers also collect a per diem of $236 a day, according to Ballotpedia's 2025 table of state legislative salaries.

That salary places California second among the 20 states in this selection, behind New York. California is one of the five legislatures that Ballotpedia names as its top five by pay.

The two leading states differ on the daily payment. California's per diem is larger than New York's, even though its base salary is smaller.

The per diem sits outside the $132,703 figure. It is set as a daily rate rather than an annual sum. Ballotpedia's table does not say how many days it applies. That means there is no annual per diem total to report. A California lawmaker's full yearly pay cannot be read from the table alone.

Ballotpedia classifies California's legislature as full time.

Pennsylvania pays its state lawmakers an annual base salary of $110,015.54, according to Ballotpedia's 2025 comparison of state legislative salaries. Members also receive a per diem of $198 a day.

Pennsylvania is one of the five states Ballotpedia names as its top five by legislative pay. Within this 20-state selection, only New York and California set a higher annual base salary.

Pennsylvania's legislature is classified as full time.

Ballotpedia lists the salary and the per diem as separate items. The $110,015.54 base figure does not include the daily payment. That payment comes on top of the salary.

Ballotpedia gives a single salary figure for Pennsylvania. It does not list separate amounts for each chamber, as it does for some other states.

Pennsylvania's lawmakers serve in two chambers. The General Assembly is made up of a House of Representatives and a State Senate.

Members of the General Assembly are not subject to term limits. They can keep seeking reelection without a cap on how many terms they serve.

Illinois state legislators earn a base salary of $93,712 a year. That places Illinois fourth among the five legislatures that Ballotpedia's 2025 comparison of state legislative salaries names as its top five by pay.

Within that group, Illinois sits below Pennsylvania's $110,015.54 and above Alaska's $84,000. Those two states hold the third and fifth spots.

Illinois lawmakers also receive a per diem of $178 a day. Ballotpedia lists this daily payment separately from the annual base pay. The table gives it as one flat rate, with no distance tiers or residency conditions attached.

That flat rate differs from the per diem rules in several other full-time legislatures in this selection. Massachusetts and Michigan pay no per diem. Ohio pays none during session. Alaska and Hawaii tie their daily payments to where a lawmaker lives.

Ballotpedia classifies the Illinois legislature as full time.

The table does not say how many days a year Illinois lawmakers collect the per diem. Without that count, the daily rate cannot be combined with the $93,712 salary to produce a yearly total.

Alaska pays its state legislators a base salary of $84,000 a year. Its legislature is classified as full time. Lawmakers from outside Juneau also collect a per diem of $307 a day. Lawmakers who live in Juneau receive no per diem.

That salary puts Alaska fifth among the 10 higher-paying states in this selection. It sits behind New York, California, Pennsylvania and Illinois. It sits ahead of Massachusetts, Hawaii, Ohio and Michigan.

The per diem rule creates two levels of compensation inside one legislature. Every member draws the same $84,000 base salary. A member from outside Juneau adds $307 for each day the per diem applies. A colleague from Juneau draws the salary alone.

Alaska's $307 daily rate is the largest per diem among the 20 states in this selection. South Carolina's $240.07 and Virginia's $237 come next.

The $84,000 figure covers base salary only. The per diem is a separate payment made on top of it. Eligibility for that payment depends on where a member lives.

Massachusetts pays its state legislators an annual base salary of $82,044.31. Ballotpedia's 2025 table lists no per diem for them and counts the legislature as full time.

That salary places Massachusetts sixth among the 10 higher-paying states in this selection. It sits below Alaska and above Hawaii.

Ballotpedia's own named top five ends with Alaska. Massachusetts is therefore the highest-paying state here that falls outside the source's named top five. Its sixth-place position comes from sorting the table's annual figures, not from a ranking Ballotpedia publishes.

A per diem is a daily allowance some states pay on top of a lawmaker's salary. In those states, yearly pay can rise above the base salary on the days a legislator qualifies for the allowance. The table shows no such rate for Massachusetts. Its base salary is the only pay figure the table lists for the state's lawmakers.

Massachusetts is not alone in this among the higher-paying states in this selection. Michigan also lists no per diem. Ohio lists none during session. New York and California, by contrast, pair their salaries with daily rates.

The full-time label puts Massachusetts alongside every other state in the upper group except Alabama. Alabama runs a hybrid legislature.

Hawaii pays its state legislators an annual base salary of $74,160. Members who live off Oahu also receive a per diem of $225 a day.

Ballotpedia's 2025 salary table lists both figures and classifies Hawaii's legislature as full time.

Whether a member gets the per diem depends on where that legislator lives. A member whose home is on an island other than Oahu qualifies for the $225 daily rate. A member who lives on Oahu gets no per diem and draws the base salary alone.

Under that rule, two Hawaii lawmakers with the same base salary can end up with different total pay. The difference comes from their home island.

Among the 10 states in the upper half of this selection, Hawaii's $74,160 salary places seventh. It falls below Massachusetts and above Ohio.

Ohio pays its state lawmakers an annual base salary of $72,343, according to Ballotpedia's 2025 comparison of state legislative salaries. Ohio legislators receive no per diem during session.

That salary ranks eighth among the 10 higher-paying states in this selection. When the source's annual figures are sorted from highest to lowest, Ohio sits just below Hawaii and just above Michigan.

Ballotpedia classifies Ohio's legislature as full time. Nine of the 10 higher-paying states in this selection carry that label. Alabama, which the source lists as hybrid, is the only exception.

Ohio's per diem entry sets it apart from most of the higher-paying group. Seven of those 10 states list some form of daily allowance on top of base salary. In Alaska and Hawaii, that allowance goes only to some members. Massachusetts and Michigan are the other two that list no per diem.

The wording of Ohio's entry differs from those two states. Massachusetts and Michigan are listed with no per diem at all. Ohio's entry specifies no per diem during session. The 2025 table does not say what, if anything, Ohio lawmakers receive outside session.

For session work, that leaves the base salary as the only pay the table records for an Ohio lawmaker. In California and Pennsylvania, by contrast, a daily allowance comes on top of a larger base salary.

The table also gives Ohio a single salary figure. Virginia lists different amounts for its delegates and its senators.

Ohio's salary exceeds every base salary in the lower half of this selection. That includes Rhode Island's, the highest in that group.

A missing per diem is not limited to the higher-paying states. Rhode Island and New Hampshire, both part-time legislatures in the lower group, also list none. In those states, no daily allowance sits on top of a much smaller base salary.

Ohio's legislature is the Ohio General Assembly. It has two chambers, the Ohio House of Representatives and the Ohio Senate.

Michigan pays its state legislators a base salary of $71,685 a year, with no per diem on top. Its legislature is full time.

That salary puts Michigan ninth among the 10 higher-paying states in this 20-state selection, which is sorted from Ballotpedia's 2025 table. Ohio ranks just above it, at $72,343. Alabama ranks just below it, at $62,212.

Michigan is also the lowest-paid full-time legislature among the 20 states here. Alabama, the next state down, runs a hybrid legislature. Every state in the lower 10 is hybrid or part time.

Without a per diem, the $71,685 salary is all of the pay Ballotpedia lists for a Michigan lawmaker. A per diem is a daily payment made on top of salary. New York, California, Pennsylvania and Illinois all pay one to their full-time legislators. Michigan does not.

Two other states in the upper group take a similar approach. Massachusetts lists no per diem. Ohio pays none during session.

The absence of a per diem also means Michigan's listed pay does not change with where a lawmaker lives. In Alaska, the daily allowance depends on whether a member lives in Juneau. In Hawaii, it goes only to members who live off Oahu. Alabama's depends on distance and trip length. Michigan's entry carries no such variation. A member from any part of the state receives the same listed figure.

Alabama pays its state legislators a base salary of $62,212 a year. It is the only hybrid legislature among the 10 higher-paying states in this selection. The other nine are all full time.

The hybrid label comes from the National Conference of State Legislatures. NCSL sorts legislatures into full-time, hybrid and part-time groups based on how much of a full-time job the legislative work takes.

NCSL found that hybrid legislators spend about 74% of a full-time job on legislative work. Average pay for the hybrid group was about $41,110.

Alabama's base salary sits above that hybrid average. The two figures come from different years, though. The NCSL average dates from 2021, while the $62,212 salary comes from Ballotpedia's 2025 table.

The hybrid label does not settle where a state's pay lands. Texas, South Carolina, Nebraska, North Carolina, Louisiana and Virginia also run hybrid legislatures, and all six sit in the lower half of this selection. Alabama shares their classification, yet its salary places it among the higher-paying states.

Alabama does not pay a single flat per diem. The daily allowance depends on distance and trip length, including whether a trip involves an overnight stay.

Trips of under six hours carry no per diem at all. A trip lasting six to 12 hours pays $12.75.

A trip of more than 12 hours without an overnight stay pays $34. A trip that includes one night away pays $85.

For trips of two or more nights, the rate rises to $100 a day. The structure ties the allowance to time spent away. A short trip earns nothing, while a multi-night stay draws the highest rate.

Among the 10 higher-paying states here, Alabama is the only one whose per diem varies with trip length. New York, California, Pennsylvania and Illinois each pay one daily rate. Massachusetts and Michigan pay no per diem. Ohio pays none during session.

Rhode Island pays its state lawmakers an annual base salary of $19,817, with no per diem on top. Ballotpedia's 2025 salary table classifies the Rhode Island legislature as part time.

With no per diem, that salary is the only regular pay the table records for a Rhode Island legislator. Days spent in session do not add a separate daily payment to it.

Among the 10 lower-paying states in this selection, Rhode Island has the highest base salary. It pays more than Virginia pays either its delegates or its senators. It also pays more than Louisiana's base salary, before Louisiana's separate expense allowance is considered.

The comparison changes once daily payments enter the picture. Several states in the lower group pay smaller salaries and then add a daily payment. Texas, South Carolina, North Carolina and Nebraska all follow that pattern. Rhode Island takes the opposite approach and puts all of its recorded pay into the salary itself.

Rhode Island is one of two states in the lower group with no per diem at all. New Hampshire is the other. In the upper group, Massachusetts and Michigan also pay no per diem, and Ohio pays none during session. All three run full-time legislatures.

Rhode Island is also one of four part-time legislatures in this selection. The others are South Dakota, New Hampshire and New Mexico. Of those four, Rhode Island pays the highest base salary.

Politico reviewed 2018 financial disclosures from state legislators whose forms were available, and Ballotpedia cites that review. Of 117 men, 42, or 36%, had other jobs. Six of 49 women also had other jobs.

As Ballotpedia presents them, those counts are not specific to Rhode Island. They show that a share of state legislators held other jobs in 2018. They do not show how many members of the Rhode Island legislature hold outside work.

Virginia pays its delegates $17,640 a year and its senators $18,000, according to Ballotpedia's 2025 salary table. Virginia legislators also collect a per diem of $237 a day.

Virginia is the only state among the 20 in this selection with two base salaries in its table entry. Senators receive the higher amount. Delegates receive the lower one.

Sorting the states requires one figure for each. This selection uses the delegates' $17,640 to place Virginia.

The choice of figure does not move Virginia. On either salary, the state sits above Louisiana and below Rhode Island. That makes Virginia the second-highest payer among the 10 lower-paying states covered here. Even the senators' $18,000 falls short of Rhode Island's base salary.

Ballotpedia classifies Virginia's legislature as hybrid. Six of the 10 lower-paying states in this selection run hybrid legislatures, and Virginia is one of them. The other four in that group are part time.

The table lists Virginia's $237 per diem as a single daily rate. Some states in this selection scale their per diem by how far a legislator lives from the Capitol. Virginia's entry attaches no distance or residency condition to its rate.

The per diem is a separate payment from base pay. It is not part of the $17,640 paid to delegates or the $18,000 paid to senators.

This slide falls short of the 300-word minimum. The source gives Virginia only its two base salaries, a per diem and a legislature type, and no approved supplementary details cover Virginia.

Louisiana pays its state legislators a base salary of $16,800 a year. Each lawmaker also receives a $6,000 annual expense allowance, which Ballotpedia's 2025 comparison of state legislative salaries lists apart from the salary.

The two payments serve different purposes. Salary is compensation for holding the office. The $6,000 is an expense payment, and it is unvouchered. Legislators do not have to back it with receipts or itemized claims to receive it.

That distinction shapes how Louisiana compares with other states. Its position in this 20-state selection rests on the $16,800 salary alone. The allowance plays no part in the ordering.

Louisiana also pays a per diem of $178 a day. A per diem is a third kind of payment, separate from both the salary and the expense allowance. It is paid for days of legislative work rather than as a fixed annual amount. The per diem grows with the number of working days, while the salary and the allowance stay fixed.

Illinois uses the same $178 daily rate. Illinois runs a full-time legislature with a far higher base salary, so an identical per diem can sit inside very different pay structures.

Louisiana's legislature is classified as hybrid.

Among the 10 lower-paying states in this selection, Louisiana's base salary ranks third from the top. Only Virginia and Rhode Island pay more in base salary within that group. South Dakota, at $16,348, sits just below Louisiana.

South Dakota pays its state legislators an annual base salary of $16,348. Its legislature is classified as part time, and members who live more than 50 miles away also receive a per diem of $178 a day.

The salary is the fixed part of the package. Every member receives the same base amount, whether home is a short drive from the statehouse or at the far end of the state.

The per diem works differently in South Dakota. The $178 daily rate goes only to legislators whose homes sit more than 50 miles away. Members who live closer receive the base salary alone.

That split means two South Dakota lawmakers serving in the same session can take home different totals from the job. The difference turns on where each member lives.

The per diem is also a daily rate rather than a yearly sum. What an eligible member collects depends on how many days that member is paid for. Ballotpedia's table does not give that count. Without it, the per diem cannot be converted into an annual figure.

The part-time label comes from the National Conference of State Legislatures, which sorts legislatures into full-time, hybrid and part-time groups. Its report dated July 28, 2021, found that part-time legislators devote about 57 percent of a full-time job to legislative work. That is more than half of a standard workload.

The same report put average pay for part-time legislators at about $18,449. South Dakota's base salary sits below that average. The two figures come from different years, though. The NCSL average dates from 2021, while South Dakota's salary comes from Ballotpedia's 2025 data.

The NCSL report also found that part-time legislators typically need another source of income beyond their legislative pay. That finding describes part-time legislatures as a group, not South Dakota's members in particular.

North Carolina pays its state legislators an annual base salary of $13,951 and a per diem of $104 a day, according to Ballotpedia's 2025 table of state legislative salaries. Ballotpedia classifies the state's legislature as hybrid.

The two figures measure different things. The salary is a fixed yearly amount. The per diem is a separate daily payment on top of it.

Ballotpedia does not say how many days a year the per diem covers. A North Carolina lawmaker's full-year pay cannot be worked out from these two figures alone.

The daily rate is lower than the salary ranking might suggest. Several states in this selection pay every member the same flat daily rate, without distance or residence conditions. Among those states, North Carolina's $104 is the lowest.

The base salary sits near the middle of the selection's lower group. Among the 10 lower-paying states here, North Carolina ranks sixth from the bottom. Its salary sits above Nebraska's $12,000. It falls below South Dakota's $16,348.

The hybrid label does not settle where a state's pay lands. Three other hybrid legislatures in this selection, Texas, South Carolina and Nebraska, pay lower base salaries than North Carolina. Virginia, Louisiana and Alabama, also hybrid, pay more.

The two states below Texas in the lower group, New Mexico and New Hampshire, both run part-time legislatures. North Carolina's hybrid legislature pays more in base salary than either of them.

Nebraska pays its state legislators an annual base salary of $12,000. Any recommended change to that salary must win voter approval before it can take effect.

That rule means the salary cannot rise through a commission report or a legislative vote alone. Nebraska voters hold the final say over what their legislators earn.

Ballotpedia's 2025 salary table classifies Nebraska's legislature as hybrid. Hybrid legislatures fall between full-time and part-time bodies.

Within the 10 lower-paying states in this selection, Nebraska sits in the middle. Its salary is higher than South Carolina's and lower than North Carolina's.

The salary is only one part of a Nebraska legislator's compensation. Lawmakers also collect a per diem. The daily rate depends on how far they live from the Capitol.

Legislators who live within 50 miles of the Capitol receive $68 a day. Those who live farther away receive $178 a day.

The distance line splits Nebraska's lawmakers into two groups for daily allowances. The $12,000 salary is the same for all of them. A legislator who lives far from the Capitol draws the higher rate for each day the per diem applies.

South Dakota also draws a line at 50 miles. There, the distance decides whether a legislator gets a per diem at all. In Nebraska, every legislator gets one, at one of two rates.

South Carolina pays its state legislators an annual base salary of $10,400, according to Ballotpedia's 2025 salary table. Lawmakers also collect a per diem of $240.07 a day.

That salary places South Carolina near the bottom of this 20-state selection. Among the 10 lower-paying states covered here, only New Mexico, New Hampshire and Texas set a smaller annual base salary.

The daily allowance points the other way. South Carolina's per diem of $240.07 is the largest daily rate among those 10 states. Virginia pays $237 a day, and Texas pays $221.

The South Carolina rate also tops California's $236 a day. California pairs that per diem with a base salary of $132,703. Across all 20 states in this selection, only Alaska's rate for legislators who live outside Juneau is higher.

A per diem is a daily payment made on top of salary. Ballotpedia's table gives no session lengths. It therefore does not show how many days qualify for the payment. Any yearly per diem total for a South Carolina legislator would be a guess.

That gap shapes how South Carolina compares with other states on this list. The salary is a fixed annual amount. The per diem varies with the number of qualifying days. Ordering by base salary captures only the fixed part of the pay.

Rhode Island pays a larger base salary than South Carolina and no per diem. Salary order alone therefore cannot show which of the two states pays its lawmakers more over a year. Texas sets both a smaller salary and a smaller daily rate than South Carolina. New Mexico sets no salary at all and a lower daily rate.

The state's legislature is classed as hybrid, a category that sits between full-time and part-time legislatures. Six other states in this selection share that classification: Alabama, Virginia, Louisiana, North Carolina, Nebraska and Texas. The salary table does not say how South Carolina sets its $10,400 figure.

Texas pays its state legislators an annual base salary of $7,200 and a per diem of $221 a day, according to Ballotpedia's 2025 comparison of state legislative salaries. Ballotpedia classifies the Texas legislature as hybrid.

That salary puts Texas third from the bottom among the 20 states in this selection. Only New Mexico and New Hampshire list a lower annual base salary.

The per diem ranks higher. Among the 10 lower-paying states in this selection, only South Carolina and Virginia list a higher daily rate than Texas.

The $221 is paid per day, separately from the salary. The annual salary on its own therefore leaves out part of what Texas pays its legislators.

Ballotpedia's table does not say how many days a year Texas legislators collect the per diem. Without that number, the source does not support a yearly total for it. It also does not show how the per diem adds up against the $7,200 salary over a full year.

New Hampshire pays its state legislators $100 a year and no per diem. Its legislature is classified as part time.

With no daily allowance added, that $100 is the full amount the state pays a member for legislative service in a year.

In this 20-state selection, only New Mexico ranks below New Hampshire on annual base salary. New Mexico's place at the bottom comes from setting no salary at all. New Hampshire does set one, but it is a nominal sum.

Those lawmakers serve in the General Court, the name for New Hampshire's legislature as a whole. It has two chambers, a House of Representatives and a Senate.

Members of the General Court face no limit on how many terms they may serve.

New Mexico pays its state legislators no salary. It is the one state in Ballotpedia's 2025 salary table that sets no base pay for lawmakers. Its legislators receive a per diem of $202 a day instead.

With a base salary of $0, New Mexico sits at the bottom of the 20 states in this selection. The $202 figure is a daily allowance, not a salary. Ballotpedia lists it separately from base pay.

The absence of a salary is written into state law at the highest level. New Mexico's constitution provides that legislators are not paid a salary.

Ballotpedia classifies New Mexico's legislature as part time.

The per diem is set as a daily rate rather than a fixed yearly sum. Ballotpedia's table does not show how many days that rate is paid in a year. It therefore does not show what a New Mexico lawmaker collects in total.

'We don't have enough time to recover:' Is the NBA game outpacing the players?

📅 Nov 03, 2026  |  Category: Business  |  Read Source →

Standing near half-court with a towel draped over his shoulders, Thunder center Isaiah Hartenstein attempted to drum up encouragement for his rookie deputy Aday Mara as he approached the bench. Teammates Micah Peavy and Bennett Stirtz waited to do the same, while occasionally tilting their heads back at the jumbotron, which flashed a deficit of nearly 20 points. Veteran guard Alex Caruso, dressed in street clothes, could only clap his hands in hopes of bringing a jolt of energy that simply would not come in a 119-98 loss to the Rockets — an unimaginable, disheartening new normal.

The 2026-27 season kicked off a fortnight ago, but Oklahoma City has already endured a campaign's worth of misfortune. Jalen Williams, lost to a fractured left fibula and a dislocated ankle. Cason Wallace, lost to a torn left labrum. Chet Holmgren, lost to a right patellar tendon tear. A tight-knit group that has always preached the importance of being where their feet are now is struggling to find its footing. A team that stood atop basketball's pinnacle less than 18 months ago now is dealing with questions about ever seriously challenging again, despite still boasting the reigning MVP and a former Coach of the Year. It's a unique type of hoops purgatory the organization hasn't experienced before.

"The morale's been pretty good all things considered," coach Mark Daigneault said on Saturday. "A week ago, the circumstances were a lot different than they are today. We understand that. We try to have an approach that can transcend these circumstances and the guys have done a great job. Obviously, the challenge is a little different now than it was prior to those injuries, but we have to recalibrate to what the challenge is and attack it a day and a game at a time."

While injuries have always been a part of the game, especially when competing at the highest level of the sport, the widespread volume and degree of ailments is troubling. One team being suddenly down 60% of its projected starting lineup is one thing, albeit a needle-in-a-haystack type probability. But the list of unavailables — Anthony Edwards, Tyrese Haliburton, Ja Morant, Ben Simmons, Jaren Jackson Jr., Paolo Banchero, Jalen Duren, Kawhi Leonard, Jalen Suggs and others — is growing, with various timelines ranging from multiple weeks to multiple months. Stars and teams are also being cautious with back-to-backs, whether due to early load management or having returned from long-term injuries. (The Pacers had 10 players out against the Hawks on Monday night, the second night of a back-to-back, citing a combination of rest and injury management.)

But the games aren't slowing down for anyone. The season has become streamlined to some degree, with the shortening of training camp/preseason (the typical ramp-up period) and the insertion of the NBA Cup. It's clearly taken a toll on the players, which in turn takes a toll on the overall product. And there doesn't seem to be an immediate fix for cramming 82 games into a six-month schedule.

Rockets guard Marcus Smart, who's dealt with serious injuries over his 12-year career, had an honest take on the growing epidemic.

"The level of basketball has become so extreme on the human body and we don't have enough time to recover like we used to," Smart said. "When I first came in the league, training camp and preseason were damn near three weeks, multiple games to get your body ready for an 82-game season. Now, depending on how far you go in the playoffs, you might get two months off? It's tough on the body.

"People don't understand; you're flying, your body's inflamed, you're landing in different time zones, go to sleep late and gotta be up early. Run as hard as you can, jump as high and fast as you can and be as physical as you can. All you're doing is asking yourself for injuries. But we signed up for it, and have to figure out a way to minimize it. Some of our best players are getting hurt and it's very unfortunate. We all want to be out here doing what we love."

There is a long list of sports scientists, doctors, data analysts and professionals who will attempt to dissect such a rapid rise of injuries in a short amount of time. But simply watching every night, the games look more rigorous, more demanding and more taxing.

Possessions are up, with the Chicago Bulls topping out at 110.2 a night, up from 107 courtesy of the 2025-26 Miami Heat. League average pace from the previous campaign is now hovering over the bottom third. The game has also become more aggressive. Last season's Pistons team, thought to be one of the more physical units around, committed 22 fouls per game. That same figure would rank 18th today. More teams are comfortable sending bodies to the offensive glass, with the Milwaukee Bucks' 33 chances per night a whopping 10 marks higher than last season's No. 1. On the defensive end, no team averaged double digits in contested rebound rate last season — there are eight such teams now. Average team speed and distance are up, too.

"There's not just one straightforward answer," said Jeff Stotts, injury analyst and owner of InStreetClothes.com. "I think you're seeing younger players get injured with a higher frequency because they're likely to have more miles on their legs before they get to the NBA. … I think the game has outpaced the player. And now you're asking your bigger, stronger athletes to also cover more ground, not only work on accelerators but also their brakes. Asking our big guys to stretch their games all the way out to the 3-point line. All those things increase the chances and likelihood of injuries occurring along the way."

Last season, only three players averaged more than 36 minutes a night — Amen Thompson, Kevin Durant and Luka Dončić. That list has since nearly tripled in size. The aforementioned list of injured stars sans Haliburton also has another thing in common: all of their usage rates were lower than the previous season. What that means is while you may be on the ball less, you're off the ball more — relocating, cutting, changing direction and using your body in other ways that still can accumulate pressure and load.

A good indicator of injury prevention is measuring an athlete's workload against their overall fitness level. Essentially the more they're asked to do physically, the more time they need to recover.

"We're in this constant back and forth between rest and acute-to-chronic load ratio, where we're trying to minimize spikes," Stotts said. "Sudden increases in load on the body in relationship to the overall conditioning of the athlete. The teams that have found success have found ways to quantify the data in a way that helps buy-in. Some teams won't rest players; others will build it in. There's no great cure."

Is such a spike in absences this early into the season enough to warrant an alternative look at scheduling or the larger umbrella of how the sport is played altogether? No magic wand can guarantee a clean bill of health, but the games, pace and aggression are increasing in frequency — and the players, whom the entire business model and ecosystem revolves around, are suffering because of it.

Google dismantles botnet secretly running on 1.3 million Android TV boxes

📅 Nov 03, 2026  |  Category: Business  |  Read Source →

Google has cut ties with the company behind the botnet campaign.

Google’s Threat Intelligence Group says it has shut down a massive botnet called x.cpe. At its height, x.cpe ran on more than 1.3 million Android TV devices, and unusually, these were Google-certified boxes. Google says its screening process missed the malware due to “obfuscation techniques” employed by Shenzhen-based Videostrong Technology.

In a detailed blog post, the Threat Intelligence Group explains how the x.cpe botnet evolved quickly from a single-stage dropper into a modular three-stage malware platform that sold access to third parties. Manufacturers must have their devices certified if they want access to Google apps and branding, a process that is supposed to catch threats like this. However, Videostrong’s compromised boxes, mostly sold under the MECOOL brand, evaded detection by using a backdoor to remotely install the malware after the devices were set up and online.

After devices appeared online, a stub application called x.cpe or com.oemservice would phone home. This package did not have any malicious capabilities of its own, but it had the system-level permissions necessary to install malware modules at the operator’s behest. It also disabled Play Protect, which would have otherwise flagged the silently installed packages as malicious.

Google found most of the boxes were running residential proxies for routing large volumes of nefarious traffic or modules that engaged in ad fraud schemes. Selling access to this botnet was probably a big business. Videostrong also had full access to the devices, allowing operators to run terminal commands, capture the screen, and probe other devices on the local network. Google reportedly observed Videostrong doing this in real time.

Google worked with several other organizations to disrupt the x.cpe network, including Japan’s National Police Agency, Synthient, and Trend Micro. Google says it has taken legal action to shut down the domains and servers Videostrong used to operate and install its malware. It also went after command-and-control servers operated by Videostrong that were connected to other malware campaigns. Google estimates Videostrong has been cut off from about 10 million compromised devices.

This action should make the affected devices safe to use, but probably not as safe as they should be. Play Protect is still disabled, and the inert malware modules are still installed. Since they are loaded as system apps, users cannot easily remove them.

Synthient has published a list of 182 device model names known to be included in the botnet. Anyone who has purchased a MECOOL or white label Android TV box of unknown origin should check it. Trend Micro counted a total of 1,472,554 infected devices, somewhat higher than Google’s estimate. The overwhelming majority were in Brazil, Malaysia, Iraq, Mexico, and Taiwan, but tens of thousands were also online in the US, Germany, the UK, Australia, and Canada.

Google determined the operation was not the work of a rogue actor, so it has ended its relationship with Videostrong. The company, as well as any subsidiaries, will not be permitted to use Google’s Android apps and services.

Google also notes that it is updating its certification process to close the loophole that allowed Videostrong to operate its botnet on certified devices. Android is open source, though, and there are countless uncertified Android-based TV boxes out there. Videostrong could continue to sell malicious hardware under other brands without Google apps. But it would still have to rebuild its malware operation from scratch.

Ars Technica has been separating the signal from the noise for over 25 years. With our unique combination of technical savvy and wide-ranging interest in the technological arts and sciences, Ars is the trusted source in a sea of information. After all, you don’t need to know everything, only what’s important.

‘La Bola Negra’ Becomes Spain’s Third Biggest Spanish Hit Ever as Its Box Office Surges 8% Above Pre-Pandemic Average

📅 Nov 03, 2026  |  Category: Business  |  Read Source →

Rentrak figures unveiled at Valladolid’s Merci market show indie distributors gaining share as cinema admissions rise 29% through September

Javier Calvo and Javier Ambrossi’s “La Bola Negra” has become the third highest-grossing Spanish film of all time at Spain’s domestic box office, earning an estimated €36.7 million ($41.0 million) through Nov. 1, according to Rentrak figures.

Only Emilio Martínez-Lázaro’s blockbuster comedy “Ocho apellidos vascos” (“Spanish Affair,” $61.8 million) and J.A. Bayona’s disaster survival drama “Lo imposible” (“The Impossible,” $47.3 million) have generated more theatrical revenue among Spanish productions. Related Stories Netflix Touts International Success Amid Content Leadership Shake-Up, Eyes More Broadcaster Deals After TF1 and Globo Pacts 'KPop Demon Hunters,' 'Squid Game' Stars Fete Netflix's Korea Decade as Ted Sarandos Teases 'Tantara,' 'All of Us Are Dead' Season 2

Distributed by independent outfit Elastica Films, the Federico García Lorca-inspired drama, starring Penélope Cruz and Glenn Close, has sold around 5.4 million tickets, emerging as Spain’s biggest homegrown hit of 2026.

Its success comes amid a sharp recovery for the Spanish theatrical market. Box office revenue surged 40% year-on-year in the first 41 weeks of 2026, reaching €489.4 million ($544.8 million) through Oct. 11, according to figures presented by Rentrak at Valladolid’s Merci independent film market.

That puts Spanish box office revenue 8% above the comparable pre-pandemic average for 2017-2019.

Spain has also outperformed the global market. Through September, its box office was up 35% year-on-year, compared with an 11% increase worldwide.

‘La Bola Negra,’ ‘Torrente for President’ Deliver Major Hits

A significant part of Spain’s surge has come from homegrown movies.

“La Bola Negra,” Santiago Segura’s “Torrente for President” and animated feature “Tadeo Jones and the Magic Lamp” account for roughly 60% of Spanish films’ box office this year. That said, around a dozen domestic productions have also surpassed $1.1 million in theatrical revenue.

“La Bola Negra” has surpassed the lifetime domestic grosses of both “Ocho apellidos catalanes,” (“Spanish Affair 2”) the 2015 sequel to Spain’s biggest homegrown hit, and “Torrente Presidente,” another major box office success of 2026.

“Before its release, ‘La Bola Negra’ had all the ingredients to be a major hit, but there was no way to foresee the scale of its success,” Rentrak Spain general manager David Rodríguez said. “Above all, its directing duo is a recognized brand in its own right, capable of mobilizing audiences and capturing the latest social trends and sensibilities.”

Its ultimate box office potential remains difficult to gauge, Rodríguez added, although possible Goya and Oscar nominations could extend its theatrical run.

Rentrak’s Cinequest research sheds light on the film’s unusually broad appeal.

Among respondents surveyed about “La Bola Negra,” 39.2% were under 35, compared with 25.1% for dramas overall. Women represented 62.1%.

The film also led Cinequest’s rankings for converting advance audience interest into definite theatrical viewing intention among 55 titles distributed by members of independent distributors association Adicine.

Although Cinequest detected strong interest and viewing intentions before release, the eventual scale of the success exceeded what those indicators could predict.

“It’s brought in many more young people, who are talking about it among themselves. The word of mouth has been tremendous,” said Rentrak client insights manager Joaquín Vegas.

“Regardless of a film’s subject or genre, the secret is finding a good story that audiences can connect with and make their own,” he added.

Vegas also praised Elastica’s promotional campaign, which helped build awareness and interest ahead of the release.

Spain’s Five Highest-Grossing Spanish Films – By Nov. 1, 2026

“Spanish Affair” (2014) — €55.4 million ($61.8 million)

“The Impossible” (2012) — €42.4 million ($47.3 million)

“La Bola Negra” (2026) — €36.7 million ($41.0 million)

“Spanish Affair 2” (2015) — €35.5 million ($39.6 million)

“Torrente for President” (2026) — €28.1 million ($31.4 million)

For Rodríguez, Spain’s recovery has been driven by a more consistent flow of box office hits throughout the year.

“After 2020, one film would take everything and the rest got nothing. Not anymore. The market is broadening,” he said.

Seven films have crossed the $22.3 million mark this year, while more than 50 have grossed over $2.2 million. Weekly box office receipts have regularly exceeded $11.1 million.

Hollywood blockbusters have helped drive the recovery. “Spider-Man: Brand New Day” and Christopher Nolan’s “The Odyssey” have attracted 5.4 million and five million spectators, respectively, in Spain.

Other major contributors include “Michael,” “The Super Mario Galaxy Movie” and “Toy Story 5.”

Despite the improving box office, cinema attendance has yet to return to pre-pandemic levels.

Admissions rose 29% between January and September compared with the same period in 2025, but remained 13% below the 2017-2019 average.

Cinequest suggests that much of the improvement comes from existing moviegoers attending more frequently, rather than from a substantial influx of new spectators.

“It’s not so much that people who didn’t go to the movies have started going, but that those who already went are going more often,” Vegas explained.

“There is life beyond Hollywood: almost 70% of global box office comes from outside North America, and local films are very important,” Rodríguez said.

For Rodríguez, the success of “La Bola Negra” also demonstrates the commercial potential of Spain’s independent distribution sector.

“Any independent distributor today is perfectly capable of handling major films and achieving fantastic box office results,” he said.

The improving market has brought substantial gains for members of Adicine, the association representing many of Spain’s leading independent film distributors.

Between January and September 2026, Adicine members generated $63.5 million in box office revenue, a 45% increase over the corresponding period in 2025.

Admissions rose 43% to 8.6 million, despite a 5% reduction in the number of films released.

Their combined box office market share increased from 11.5% to 12.4% over those same nine-month periods — figures that capture only the opening days of “La Bola Negra’s” theatrical run, before most of its remarkable October box office performance.

United Airlines will expand to northern Mexico next spring despite a cloudy outlook.

The Star Alliance carrier will return to Chihuahua Airport (CUU) in northern Mexico from Houston’s George Bush Intercontinental Airport (IAH) beginning March 28, 2027.

Edibray Gómez Gallegos, the secretary of tourism of Chihuahua state, said the new route will increase connectivity for the state and "reflects growing interest among North American travelers in discovering the many tourist attractions Chihuahua has to offer."

United last served the IAH-CUU route in 2020, according to schedule data from aviation analytics firm Cirium. It will join American Airlines among U.S. carriers serving the airport.

A spacious Bombardier CRJ-550 will be used on the route. The plane, originally a 70-seat CRJ-700, is fitted with 10 first-class, 20 extra-legroom economy and 20 economy seats.

Other news: United is bringing its nicest plane (with the Polaris Studios) to a city that never sees it

The new CUU route comes as airlines around the globe have been forced to adjust to soaring fuel costs.

United CEO Scott Kirby has previously warned that United has “no hesitation to cut capacity of flights that are losing money” in 2027 if fuel prices stay elevated as expected.

For context, the average global price for a gallon of jet fuel stood at $4.56 during the week ending Oct. 30, up 3.8% from the previous month's average, according to data from trade group IATA.

For now, however, the carrier remains mostly in growth mode. This new route is the latest in a bevy of additions from United in 2027.

The airline will add at least five new routes from San Francisco International Airport (SFO), including to Charleston International Airport (CHS) in South Carolina and Richmond International Airport (RIC) in Virginia, and from O’Hare International Airport (ORD) in Chicago to L.F. Wade International Airport (BDA) in Bermuda.

And then there is United’s blockbuster 10-route summer long-haul expansion that will add destinations including Luxembourg Airport (LUX) and Ljubljana Airport (LJU) in Slovenia to its map.

It is not all growth for United next year, though. The airline will end service to Tulum International Airport (TQO) in Mexico in January.

Overall, these additions and cuts underscore United's efforts to grow its network while keeping an eye on profitability.

How American & United Airlines Are Turning 19-Year-Olds With Zero Flight Hours Into Branded Airline Pilots

📅 Nov 03, 2026  |  Category: Business  |  Read Source →

Philip Pilosian & Ceri Breeze | Shutterstock, Simple Flying

For decades, the traditional route to an airline cockpit followed a familiar pattern - aspiring pilots learned to fly independently, paid for ratings however they could, accumulated hours through flight instruction or charter work, and then competed for regional airline jobs before eventually reaching a major carrier.

The journey was expensive, uncertain, and largely disconnected from the airlines that would one day employ successful candidates. That model is now changing, with two of the largest carriers in the US becoming involved years earlier than ever before. United Airlines' Aviate program and American Airlines' Cadet Academy represent something much bigger than a response to pilot shortages.

Rather than simply recruiting experienced aviators, the two legacy carriers are increasingly manufacturing their own future workforce from the very beginning, welcoming candidates as young as 19 with no previous flight experience, financing their training, shaping their professional development, and creating airline-branded pilots long before they ever operate a scheduled passenger flight.

The emergence of structured ab-initio pilot programs marks a significant shift in how major US airlines think about recruitment. Instead of waiting for pilots to arrive after years of independent flying, both United Airlines and American Airlines have built systems that identify candidates before they have logged their first hour in an aircraft, then guide them through every major stage of their development.

This approach reflects the enormous long-term demand facing commercial aviation. Industry forecasts project that airlines around the world will require more than 600,000 new pilots over the next two decades, while United Airlines alone expects to hire more than 10,000 pilots during the next ten years.

Those figures make relying entirely on traditional recruitment increasingly risky, especially as retirements continue to reduce the existing pilot pool. Rather than treating pilot hiring as the final step in a long career journey, both airlines increasingly see training as the first stage of employment.

Candidates apply to airline-branded academies, complete standardized flight instruction, become instructors to build experience, move into affiliated regional airlines, and eventually transition into mainline operations under an established pathway. The result is a system where airlines have visibility over future employees from the very beginning, creating a far more predictable talent pipeline than previous generations ever experienced.

The academy trains hundreds of pilots each year.

Andrey_Popov | Shutterstock, Simple Flying

Perhaps the biggest attraction of these programs is the clarity they provide. Traditional flight training often required aspiring pilots to make major financial commitments without any certainty that an airline job would ultimately materialize, forcing graduates to search independently for instructing positions before competing for regional airline openings.

United Airlines' Aviate pathway and American Airlines' Cadet Academy replace much of that uncertainty with a clearly defined sequence of milestones. Students begin with private pilot training before progressing through instrument, commercial, multi-engine, and instructor certifications.

After qualifying as certified flight instructors, they accumulate the flight experience required for airline employment while teaching new students, remaining within the broader airline-sponsored ecosystem. The next step usually involves joining wholly owned or closely affiliated regional carriers.

American Airlines' process feeds pilots toward the oneworld carrier's regional subsidiary operators, including Envoy Air, PSA Airlines, and Piedmont Airlines, while United Airlines' pathway directs graduates toward participating United Express operators. Instead of repeatedly searching for new employers as experience increases, participants generally follow a planned progression.

This eventually leads them toward the mainline airline. United Airlines' pathway includes another notable benchmark before pilots can transition into the mainline operation. Aviate participants must complete 1,200 hours as pilot in command while flying for a participating regional or approved operator.

Thereafter, they become eligible to join the Star Alliance carrier as a first officer. That requirement ensures pilots arrive with meaningful operational experience while still remaining within a carefully managed development system that has followed them since their earliest training flights.

Learning to fly has always demanded a substantial financial commitment, making cost one of the largest obstacles preventing many talented candidates from pursuing airline careers. Flight training from zero experience through instructor certifications can easily reach well into six figures, particularly when housing and living expenses are included.

American Airlines has responded by integrating specialized financing directly into the Cadet Academy experience. Through American Airlines Federal Credit Union, eligible students can obtain loans of up to $148,000 covering flight training costs, housing expenses, and even additional living support.

There are no required loan payments for as long as 45 months while completing training and beginning their careers. That structure fundamentally changes the financial calculation for many applicants. Instead of assembling funding from multiple private lenders or interrupting training because of cash flow problems, students can focus on progressing through the academy with financing.

Such support is specifically designed around the unusually long timeline required for professional pilot development. United Airlines also places considerable emphasis on reducing financial barriers. Alongside financing opportunities available through training partners, the carrier, Boeing, and Chase have collectively funded millions of dollars in scholarship support for Aviate Academy students.

This includes a $3.45 million initiative designed partly to broaden access and diversify the future pilot workforce. These efforts acknowledge that expanding recruitment requires attracting candidates who previously may have considered professional aviation financially unattainable.

A closer look at the hidden factors behind the pilot shortage crisis.

While financing and guaranteed career pathways receive much of the public attention, the airlines arguably gain an equally valuable advantage through early cultural development. Instead of introducing company procedures after pilots have accumulated thousands of hours elsewhere, the airlines begin shaping professional behavior from the very first stages of training.

Students are introduced early to airline expectations surrounding Crew Resource Management, automation philosophy, communication standards, decision-making, professionalism, and operational discipline. These concepts exist throughout commercial aviation, but teaching them within an airline-specific framework allows companies to reinforce the behaviors they believe best support their own operational culture.

This creates a very different educational environment from traditional independent flight schools. This is because, rather than simply producing licensed commercial pilots who later adapt to whichever airline hires them, academy students develop while already identifying with a specific carrier's expectations and values.

Every stage, from classroom instruction through regional airline operations, reinforces those standards before candidates ever reach a cockpit. The approach also functions as an extended evaluation process, enabling airlines to observe performance, professionalism, leadership, communication, and decision-making across several years.

This acts as an alternative to relying primarily on interviews conducted shortly before employment. Technical flying skills remain essential, but the longer relationship gives recruiters a much richer understanding of how future pilots respond to training, feedback, teamwork, and operational pressures throughout their development.

One of the more interesting aspects of these programs is how effectively they transform recruitment into long-term brand building. Students are not simply learning to become airline pilots: rather, they are encouraged to think of themselves as future United Airlines or American Airlines pilots almost immediately after joining their respective academies.

That identity develops gradually throughout the structured pathway: cadets interact with airline mentors, attend company events, receive guidance from employees already flying within the network, and become familiar with company culture years before operating scheduled passenger services.

Rather than viewing regional airlines as temporary employers to leave at the earliest opportunity, participants increasingly see each career stage as part of a single journey toward the same parent airline. From the airlines' perspective, this creates a stronger organizational commitment than traditional hiring models.

Pilots who have spent years progressing through a branded system have already invested significant time, relationships, and professional identity within that company. The airline has effectively cultivated loyalty throughout the training process instead of attempting to establish it only after employment begins.

This branding strategy also differentiates the modern academies from earlier airline-sponsored recruitment initiatives. The emphasis extends beyond simply producing qualified pilots to creating individuals who already understand company expectations, recognize familiar faces within the organization, and identify personally with the airline's broader culture before reaching the mainline fleet.

The significance of these programs extends well beyond solving immediate staffing challenges. They represent a broader shift in how legacy airlines view talent development, replacing reactive recruitment with long-term workforce planning that begins almost at the moment someone decides to become a pilot.

Rather than depending on an external training ecosystem to produce candidates with varying backgrounds, United Airlines and American Airlines increasingly control much of the developmental process themselves. Standardized instruction, integrated financing, predictable career progression, and continuous evaluation produce pilots whose professional experience has unfolded almost entirely within one airline family's operational philosophy.

That level of involvement would have been unusual only a generation ago, when major airlines largely hired pilots after careers built across numerous employers, military service, charter operators, or independent flight schools. Today's model instead resembles an integrated apprenticeship stretching across many years, where technical qualifications, cultural alignment, and career progression all develop simultaneously.

For pilots, these pathways dramatically reduce many of the traditional uncertainties surrounding an aviation career. For the airlines, they accomplish something equally important by ensuring that long before a new first officer reports to operate a Boeing or Airbus aircraft. Years of training have already shaped not only how that pilot flies, but also how they think, communicate, and identify with the airline whose uniform they will eventually wear.

When I was young and interested in becoming a commercial airline pilot there was no other clearly defined way into the airlines other than through the military. I spent many hours of my youth trying to figure this out. These really young, inexperienced from the get go pilots are being pumped out rapidly by these flight schools. Is this a potentially dangerous thing?

D&D’s World of Warcraft Crossover Is the Bold Shake-Up the Game Needed

📅 Nov 03, 2026  |  Category: Business  |  Read Source →

The crossover creates opportunities for more complex characters and fights

Wizards of the Coast has been playing things safe since updating the rules of Dungeons & Dragons 5th Edition in 2024. After lead designers Jeremy Crawford and Chris Perkins left Wizards in 2025 to join Critical Role’s Darrington Press, Wizards slowed the pace of new D&D releases. Recent books have focused mostly on updating the lore and rules for the game’s most popular campaign settings and rebalancing previously published character options. D&D’s newest release bucks the trend, delivering an ambitious collection of innovative character options, monsters, and ways to use some of the game’s less developed subsystems. It just requires venturing to a new world.

Dungeons & Dragons: World of Warcraft is the first book in the Universes Beyond line, an extension of the controversial Magic: The Gathering strategy that filled the collectible card game with licensed tie-ins. Even though WoW and D&D share many of the same classes and monsters, marrying the two games is a daunting challenge that involves incorporating lore from 22 years of video game releases and 12,000 years of conflict on the planet of Azeroth. But it also provided a unique opportunity to increase D&D’s complexity.

A big part of 5E’s appeal is how simple it is to learn and play, especially at low levels. But WoW is a game about big boss fights and characters with a huge array of abilities, and D&D: World of Warcraft reflects that with an emphasis on high-level play and more tactical combat at every level.

Sometimes the translation is needlessly confusing. WoW has some heroic classes that don’t start at level 1. D&D: WoW both introduces the term and then tells players they can start at level 1 anyway. Once you can get past the confusing language, the result is a set of rich new character options that DMs might want to consider for any game. For instance, the Death Knight modifies many of the core abilities of a Paladin, going beyond the neat building blocks of a subclass to transform a holy warrior into a necromancy-powered master of the battlefield who can pull foes into melee and charge through walls on a spectral steed. Demon Hunters, a similarly dramatic reimagining of Rangers, utilize Warlock powers and gather souls to heal themselves and harm their enemies.

The extra power is necessary when facing the denizens of WoW’s dungeons. Lead designer James Wyatt and his team capture the feel of WoW’s multiphased boss fights with a mix of legendary actions, the bloodied condition that 5.5E brought back from 4E, and the mythic actions introduced in the Magic crossover campaign book Mythic Odysseys of Theros. When bloodied or dropped to zero hit points, many enemies heal and unlock devastating new abilities that can make the fight feel more desperate as it goes on.

The book does a solid job of compressing WoW’s extremely convoluted lore and storylines to then transform them into compelling adventure hooks. There are plenty of ideas for campaigns of all levels focused on fighting back the corrupting influence of the Old Gods, the rampaging Scourge horde, or the relentless wrath of the Burning Legion, but this is a book that really shines when it comes to high-level dungeons and enemies. Once D&D characters hit level 10, they gain access to powerful magic that makes travel nearly instantaneous or quickly cures most problems. This disrupts the Tolkien-inspired fantasy most D&D games are built on. But these powers perfectly fit the tone of WoW, where players are zipping across the globe to fight apocalyptic threats before teleporting back to the safety of a cozy inn. Likewise, the newly introduced Potions of Mana, which let characters recover spell slots, would break most games, but they work when parties are expected to undergo a gauntlet of tough battles to clear out a dungeon.

Beyond enhancing combat, the book boosts the utility of some of D&D’s underutilized subsystems to get across the richness of WoW’s gameplay. The Dungeon Master Guide’s Renown system is used to track a character’s reputation with WoW’s many factions so they can earn access to powerful rewards. Crafting gets a huge overhaul as well, allowing characters to use their tool proficiencies to make magical items even if they’re not spellcasters. Likewise, the foraging action taken during exploration expands to represent WoW’s gathering skills by allowing characters to search for rare materials.

It’s a shame that this isn’t a bigger book. Wyatt and his team clearly wanted to go further into depth in the crafting and gathering section and create a recipe list where each item has its own components found in certain areas of the world. Instead they mostly abstract it, but then give some specific examples, making the section feel convoluted and far too reliant on external research. Similarly, the enemies are well-crafted and flavorful, but just a bit more text covering the environmental challenges players might face exploring dungeons and the terrain in fights would go a long way to making those adventures more immersive.

WoW and D&D have so much in common that this crossover feels natural in a way that’s less likely to be true for the Star Wars Universes Beyond book scheduled for release in 2027. But D&D: WoW gives me hope that the Universes Beyond endeavor can be more than just a business tactic to tap into existing fandom, creating a place where D&D’s designers have room to experiment with mechanics viewed as too complex for the core game. Even if you’ve never played WoW, it’s worth checking out this book for fresh ideas on how to challenge your players, especially at higher levels.

The Cat in the Hat, reviewed in the style of Dr. Seuss

📅 Nov 03, 2026  |  Category: Business  |  Read Source →

Ever since I was a small Who, I have loved Dr. Seuss.

His Sneetches, the Lorax, Horton — the joy they induce!

I gobbled up books, shorts, and feature films, too.

I giggled, sang along, and even cried a boo-hoo!

But one of his heroes had a movie fall flat,

That rascally sharp dresser, the Cat in the Hat.

In 2003, Mike Myers tried in live-action.

Audiences marveled at what the duck happened.

Now comes an animated The Cat in the Hat.

Voiced by Bill Hader, this feline is fun.

He brings with him his Things One, Two, and beyond,

Plus a goldfish who wags a fin at things done wrong.

But the focus of this kid's movie is odd:

He works in the business of bringing kids joy,

They've just moved and are feeling quite sad,

But it's not enough to make them feel glad.

Of how the Cat brings mirth to kids near and far.

If that sounds convoluted, oh, just you wait.

This story gets overcrowded to a terrible state.

A mother-daughter squabble kicks off the action,

Then a MacGuffin makes for a time-travel distraction.

Erica Rivinoja and Alessandro Carloni direct and write.

Their animation is warm, delicately textured, and bright.

The voice cast has performers spirited and merry,

Like Quinta Brunson, Xochitl Gomez, and Matt Berry.

Sure, there's fun to be had in this Cat's appearance,

But too many fart jokes hurt the experience.

With an overstuffed plot and action chaotic,

The movie's message feels half-assed and quixotic.

Hader's The Cat In The Hat is certainly better than Myers'.

But so is plucking out my nose hairs with needle-nose pliers.

In the end, I can't cheer this cartoon's feline joker.

Best I can say is that this movie is mediocre.
